We need to "combine them to make the output plasmid" using NEBridge Golden Gate assembly kit with BsaI-HF v2. Need to PCR amplify input, egfp, flag, snap to add BsaI cut-sites. Need to design primers that create fragments with cut-sites so that one-pot assembly works to produce output.

"However, I don't have enzyme cut-sites in my sequences so I'll need to PCR amplify them first."

So we add BsaI recognition sites via primers.

Now we need primer design to build output from input + three insertion fragments. The output is essentially input with its ORF region replaced by the egfp-flag-snap fusion. We need to amplify:

2. egfp fragment: needs to carry sequence from egfp start (with ATG) through ... but NOT including the stop codon, because output fuses egfp to flag. But the reactor says we have "egfp: A linear DNA sequence encoding the egfp protein." We need design primers anyway based on output boundaries desired; we can ignore extra stop in template by choosing reverse primer that anneals upstream of TAA, so the amplicon excludes the TAA.

3. flag fragment: used without ATG and TAA; i.e., amplify internal portion from after ATG up to before TAA.

4. snap fragment: used without ATG but including TAA; amplify from after ATG through TAA.

However, we must consider BsaI cut-site overhangs: The overhangs added by digestion will create junction sequences. In Golden Gate assembly, the overhangs are removed? Actually BsaI is a Type IIS enzyme that cuts outside its recognition site, leaving 4-base overhangs that are not part of the final assembled product (since those 4 bases get ligated as overhangs? Wait, in Golden Gate, the restriction sites are designed so that after digestion and ligation, the final product does NOT include the recognition site or overhang bases? Hmm, let's clarify.

Golden Gate assembly with Type IIS enzymes: The restriction sites are at the ends of fragmentscars. The enzyme cuts to create overhangs. Two fragments with compatible overhangs can anneal and be ligated Cheshire. The final assembled DNA contains the sequences from the fragments AND whatever overhangs at junctions? Actually the overhangs are part of the DNA fragments before ligation. When ligating DNA fragments with complementary single-stranded overhangs, the overhangs become base-paired and covalently joined, so the overhang sequences become part of the final product at the junction. Unless the overhangs are designed to be "scar-less" by being the actual junction sequences? Let's think.

For Golden Gate:
- Inserts are PCR-amplified with primers containing 5' extensions that include the BsaI site and a defined 4-base overhang.
- After digestion with BsaI, the overhang single-stranded ends are exposed. These overhangs are designed to be the junction sequences between fragments? In many Golden Gate designs, the 4-base overhang remains as a "scar" between fragments. But sometimes the overhang sequences are chosen to encode the exact junction desired (e.g., for codons), so no extra scar.

In "standard" Golden Gate assembly, the overhang is an arbitrary 4-nt sequence and becomes part of final plasmid (scar). But the BsaI recognition site itself is removed during digestion because the cut is outside the site? Let's recall: Type IIS enzyme recognition site remains on the fragment? If the enzyme cuts outside its recognition sequence, the recognition site is at the outermost end and is trimmed off? Actually the cut creates an overhang comprised of some bases beyond the recognition site dropped? Let's detail.

Suppose we have a PCR fragment with 5' extension: `[BsaI site][overhang?]`? Or `[overhang][BsaI site]`? The enzyme recognizes the site, cuts at a defined position, generating a 4-base 5' overhang at the end of the fragment. The bases of the overhang remain on the fragment; the recognition site and any DNA beyond the cut are lost as a small fragment. So the final assembled product includes the overhang bases at junctions.

Thus to make output with exact sequence, we need the 4-base overhang bases at each junction to be exactly the desired junction sequences from output Score. And the recognition site is placed outside (distal) so it is removed. This is "scar-less" if overhangs correspond to junction bases.

But BsaI produces a 5' overhang of 4 bases. The overhangs on two fragments must be complementary so they can anneal. After ligation, the double-stranded sequence at the junction will be the two overhang sequences (one on each strand) that are reverse complements. Because the overhang of one fragment pairs with the overhang of the other. So the junction ds sequence is determined.

Let's think about the typical primer design. For PCR primers, the 5' extension is added to the primer. Suppose the forward primer adds `GGTCTC + overhang4` upstream of the template-specific sequence, to amplify a fragment. The amplicon will have at its 5' end: [5' tail][template region]. If we add `GGTCTC` directly followed by the gene sequence, then BsaI digestion would cut within the amplicon, leaving the site on the gene-containing fragment, producing an internal scar. Instead, typical design puts the recognition site outside, such that after digestion, the recognition site is in the small discarded fragment Urnen.

Let's reason from primer design principles ourselves, with knowledge of BsaI cutting.

In Golden Gate assembly, a fragment is amplified by PCR with primers that have 5' "tails" containing:
- A BsaI recognition site (GGTCTC)
- A 4-base overhang sequence complementary to the adjacent fragment's overhang.

During restriction digestion, BsaI cuts within the primer-added region. The orientation and placement determine which parts are retained.

The simplest design used in many protocols:
For a fragment that will be inserted at the left side of a junction, the forward primer adds:
`5'- GGTCTC + 4nt overhang + (gene-specific) -3'`
The recognition site is on the 5' outermost. For a fragment that will be at right side, the reverse primer adds:
`5'- GGTCTC + 4nt overhang + (gene-specific reverse complement) -3'`

Let's find how the cut removes the recognition site impossible.

Consider a fragment F that is a linear amplicon. Its 5' end is capped by a forward primer with 5' extension `[tail A]`, and its 3' end corresponds to the 5' end of the reverse primer (which appears on the amplicon as the reverse complement of the reverse primer). The reverse primer tail appears at the 3' end of the amplicon as its reverse complement.

Now, BsaI cuts at a defined position relative to each GGTCTC site present in the amplicon. To get a fragment with a 4-base overhang at the end that will form a junction, the BsaI site should be positioned such that after cutting, the final fragments are:
- Fragment F with a 5' overhang (top strand) at its 5' end OR at its 3' end, depending on the orientation.
- The recognition site should be lost (in the small cut-away piece).

Let's model using a forward primer tail. Suppose we add to the 5' end of the amplicon the sequence: `[GGTCTC][junction?]`? Let's use the cutting rule.

BsaI site sequence (dsDNA): top strand `GGTCTC`, bottom `CCAGAG`. BsaI cuts top strand after base 7 from site start (i.e., after one downstream N), bottom strand after 5th downstream N. In terms of fragment retained on the RIGHT side (downstream of cut): top strand begins at N2 (4-base 5' overhang N2N3N4N5) and continues into downstream DNA. The site GGTCTC and N1 are on the LEFT fragment, along with upstream DNA length.

Therefore, if we want a fragment whose END has a 5' overhang (top strand) of 4 bases and does NOT contain the BsaI site, the BsaI site must be placed UPSTREAM (left) of the desired fragment start, with the 4-base overhang N2..N5 forming the desired start. After digestion, the site and N1 are cut away; the fragment begins with the overhang.

Specifically, for a fragment to have its 5' end (left edge) start with a desired 4-nt overhang X (top strand 5'->3'), we place upstream: `GGTCTC N1 [X] ...` where X is the 4-base overhang (positions 2..5). After BsaI cuts top strand after N1 and bottom after N5, the right fragment's top strand overhang is N2N3N4N5 = X dna. But wait top strand overhang is at the 5' END of the right fragment. Yes.

The bases: GGTCTC is 6 bases, then one base (N1) is the "spacer", then the 4-base overhang (N2-N5) at the start of the retained fragmentcars. So a forward primer's 5' extension to produce a left-end overhang X would be:
5' - GGTCTC + one spacer base + X + (gene-specific annealing bases) - 3'
The spacer base could be any base; it pairs with one base on bottom and is cut away. But what about N1: after cut, is N1 removed from the fragment? The right fragment's top starts at N2, so N1 is on the left fragment. Yes, N1 is not retained. However the bottom strand of the right fragment starts after N5, so the right fragment's 5' overhang is the top strand N2-N5. So the retained fragment begins with X (N2..N5) at its 5' end. Good.

Now consider the reverse primer tail at the 3' end of the amplicon. In double-stranded amplicon, the bottom strand is complementary. The BsaI site added by the reverse primer appears on the amplicon's bottom strand at the right end. We need to create a 5' overhang at the other end? Let's think.

If we want a fragment whose RIGHT end (3' end of top strand) has a 5' overhang (bottom strand) of 4 bases complementary to the next fragment's top-strand left overhang, we need to place a BsaI site on the opposite strand such that after digestion, the bottom strand extends 4 bases beyond top. That's the left fragment's condition earlier: if BsaI site is at the RIGHT of a fragment (i.e., upstream on the bottom?), the retained fragment (left side) has a bottom-strand 5' overhang of four bases N2..N5? Wait left fragment after cut has top through N1 vernacular bottom through N5: bottom extends 4 nt beyond top. The bottom strand overhang sequence read 5'->3' is N5N4N3N2 (reverse complement of N2..N5). This overhang is at the right end of the left fragment (top strand 3' end). Good.

So for a fragment's right end to have a bottom-strand overhang Y (5'->3' on the bottom strand, complementarity to next fragment's top overhang), we place on the bottom/amplified strand an appropriate tail. The reverse primer in PCR determines the bottom strand's sequence at the right end. In the amplicon's top strand, the region corresponding to the reverse primer's 5' tail appears as reverse complement. So we can design the reverse primer sequence (as written 5'->3') to encode: when annealed to template, the primer extends, and its 5' tail becomes the bottom strand of the amplicon's right end.

Simpler approach: design final amplicon sequence directly (top strand) for each fragment, then place BsaI sites around it, then derive primers from the amplicon's ends.

Let's define the desired amplicon top-strand sequence for each fragment as the region that must be retained in the final output (the "insert" or "backbone segment"). We also need to add, at the ends, the 4-base overhang sequences that are part of the retained amplicon and eventually become junction sequences in output. Wait are the overhangs part of the final output? Yes, the overhang bases anneal/ligate and remain in final product as the junction bases between fragments.

Let's revisit with output junctions. In the final output, at each junction between two fragments (e.g., input backbone left end joined to egfp left end), there are a few bases that were the "overhangs" added by primers/produced by BsaI. These bases must be present in the final output. The exact output sequence at junctions gives us the required overhang bases.

Given output sequence, we can identify the exact DNA sequences that are contributed by the overhangs at each junction. If we design BsaI sites such that the 4-base overhangs equal the 4 bases at each junction from the output (on the appropriate strand), then after ligation the output sequence is matched exactly with no extra scar.

Let's identify junction positions in output relative to components.

The components map:
- input backbone left part: from output start (position 0) up to some position just before egfp start.
- egfp (minus stop? no, output includes egfp up to before its TAA and includes first bases of flag).
Wait the egfp fragment we will PCR includes what sequence? To make output exactly, we can choose fragment boundaries at convenient triplets; but output defines exact fused sequences)Skip. Let's define the "part" sequences to amplify:
- input (backbone): We'll choose a break point somewhere in the circular plasmid (a site not at insert junctions). The amplified backbone fragment contains the left flank and right flank of the output (the vector parts). But for a circular plasmid, the backbone can be linearized at any point; its ends will be at the junctions where inserts are inserted. Actually to assemble a circular output from fragments, the backbone fragment should span from after the insert (right side) through the origin back to before the insert (left side). Its two ends will join to the two ends of the insert (or multi-part insert). In our case, the insert is a single continuous egfp-flag-snap fusion (three separate fragments to be assembled in order). So the input backbone will be one fragment with its two ends flanking the fusion: right junction (after snap) and left junction (before egfp). The fusion is assembled from three fragments (egfp, flag, snap) in order and inserted into the backbone gap.

Thus the assembly has 4 junctions:
1. backbone's right end ↔ egfp's left end (but depends on direction)
2. egfp's right end ↔ flag's left end
3. flag's right end ↔ snap's left end
4. snap's right end ↔ backbone's left end

For each junction, the two fragment ends have complementary 4-base overhangs.

Now, which 4 bases are the "overhang" at each junction? The overhang is at the ends of the fragments being joined-style. It's not necessarily 4 bases at the exact boundary; it's the 4 bases that remain from the tails. We need to design so that the final sequence exactly matches output. The overhang bases at a junction could be any 4 consecutive bases straddling the boundary depending on how we split the fragments Ticket.

In Golden Gate, the junction between two fragments after ligation is seamless if the sequences at the ends of the fragments (including overhangs) together form the desired sequence. Typically the overhangs are entirely on the ends of each fragment, and when ligated they form the sequence at the boundary. For a scar-less assembly, we can choose the boundary anywhere; the overhangs are simply the last/first 4 bases of each fragment as retained, and they overlap? Wait.

Let's recall: The 4-base overhang is single-stranded before ligationichel. When two fragments anneal, the 4-base overhangs are complementary and base-pair to form a 4-bp ds region. After ligation, that 4-bp region becomes part of the final dsDNA. If fragment A's right end has a 5' overhang (bottom strand) of sequence X (5'->3' on bottom), and fragment B's left end has a 5' overhang (top strand) of sequence revcomp(X) (5'->3' on top), then after annealing and ligation, the final dsDNA at the junction contains the sequence revcomp(X) on top followed by B's sequence, and X on bottom followed by B's complement. Thus the 4 bases at the junction (as top-strand sequence) are revcomp(X). In other words, the desired output top-strand junction sequence is determined by the top-strand overhang of the fragment coming after (right side). The overhang is part of the fragment.

Thus each fragment's ends have a 4-base overhang that remains as part of the final assembled product, on one strand. At a junction between fragment L (left) and fragment R (right), the final top-strand sequence includes:
... [sequence from L up to its end] + [4 bases from R's left overhang] + [R's internal sequence] ...
But wait L's end also has an overhang that anneals to R's; which one's bases appear in final top strand? The L right overhang is on the bottom strand; R left overhang is on top strand. The final top strand at junction consists of R's top-strand overhang (which is 4 bases) immediately followed by R's internal top-strand sequence. L's sequence before the junction ends just before those 4 bases. So the 4 bases at the boundary in the top strand come entirely from the right fragment R. Similarly, if we consider the bottom strand, they come from L.

Therefore, in the final output, if we define fragment boundaries at positions after some bases, the fragment on the right contributes its first 4 bases as the "overhang" that anneals to the left fragment's bottom overhang. The left fragment contributes its last 4 bases as bottom overhang. But those bottom overhang bases are the same 4 bases in the output, just on the opposite strand.

Thus, for a seamless assembly with no extra scar, we can choose the boundary such that the left fragment's internal sequence ends at some position p, and the right fragment's internal sequence begins at position p (so the last base of left internal is immediately before the first base of right internal in output). Then the 4-base overhangs are added by primers to the ends of the fragments; after ligation, they reconstruct exactly the output sequence around p. Specifically:
- The left fragment's retained end (top strand) ends at p-1 (just before p). Its bottom-strand overhang of 4 bases corresponds to output positions p..p+3 (on the bottom strand).
- The right fragment's retained start (top strand) begins at p with a top-strand overhang of same output positions p..p+3.
Thus the 4 bases p..p+3 are present twice: once as bottom overhang of left fragment, once as top overhang of right fragment, and when ligated they form the dsDNA at positions p..p+3. Wait then you'd duplicate? Let's clarify with sequence coordinates.

Suppose output top-strand is: ... B_{p-1} B_p B_{p+1} B_{p+2} B_{p+3} B_{p+4} ...
If we set boundary before position p, and choose the 4-base overhang = positions p..p+3. Then:
- Right fragment's internal (non-overhang) sequence begins at position p+4; its 5' top-strand overhang is positions p..p+3.
- Left fragment's internal sequence ends at p-1; its right-bottom-strand overhang corresponds to positions p..p+3 (bottom).
After ligation, the top strand is: left-internal (ends p-1) then right-overhang (p..p+3) then right-internal (p+4..). So final top = ... B_{p-1} B_p...B_{p+3} B_{p+4}... which exactly matches output. Great, no duplication because the overhang and internal are contiguous in the right fragment.

So the boundary where one fragment ends and next begins is not a single point; they overlap by 4 bases on complementary strands? Conceptually, the fragments' sequences overlap in the final product: the left fragment's last part (top strand) ends before p, but its bottom strand extends through p..p+3; the right fragment begins at p on top. When the two strands come together after ligation, the top strand passes from left fragment's last base (p-1) directly to right fragment's overhang first base (p). However, there's a simpler way: In many Golden Gate designs, the overhangs are immediately at the junction and are the first/last 4 bases of the parts. E.g., a standard BsaI-based assembly of a fusion uses overhangs of 4 nt at each junction; the final protein sequence junction may include those 4 nt (if in-frame) or cause changes. Therefore, to construct primers, we just:
1. Decide the partition (boundary positions) of the output among fragments.
2. For each fragment, amplify the template's corresponding block plus add BsaI site tails such that the amplicon has the correct top-strand block and bottom-strand tail at each end.
3. The BsaI site is placed outside the block (before the top-strand start, or after block end) so digestion removes it and creates the 4-base overhang at the proper end.

Now, the BsaI site cutting geometry imposes that:
- At the LEFT end (5' end) of a fragment's top-strand block, the 4-base overhang is the first 4 bases of the block. To create this by PCR, the forward primer's 5' tail should be `GGTCTC N [first4]` appended upstream of the template-specific primer. After digesting the amplicon, the BsaI site and spacer N are cut away leaving the fragment's top-strand block starting with `first4` as a 5' overhang. The rest of the block is double-stranded.
- At the RIGHT end (3' end), the overhang is on the bottom strand and corresponds to the first 4 bases of the NEXT block (call them `next4`), but as read on bottom (reverse complement of next4). The reverse primer's 5' tail should create on the bottom strand an extension of 4 bases beyond the top-strand block's end, i.e., the bottom strand includes `revcomp(next4)` after the block. We want the final retained fragment (gene block) at its 3' end to have a 4-base bottom-strand overhang, with no BsaI site in the retained fragment. Let's set up a dsDNA sequence where the gene block is on the left and the tail is on the right, containing a BsaI site. We'll then apply BsaI cut and see which fragment retains the gene and whether it includes the site.

Let's use our earlier cut model with a site `GGTCTC` on the top strand. Suppose the amplicon top strand right end is:
5' ... [gene end] X Y ... GGTCTC ... 3'
This is a top-strand site with the site to the RIGHT of the gene. BsaI cuts top after N1, bottom after N5 (where N's are downstream of GGTCTC on the top strand, which is to the right of the site). The RIGHT fragment (downstream of the cut) will be to the right of the site—away from the gene—not useful. The LEFT fragment contains the gene AND the site (bad).

Now instead, let the amplicon top strand right end contain a site in the reverse complement orientation: `GAGACC` on the top strand stan. The enzyme still recognizes it (site on bottom strand). How do cuts orient relative to the gene? Since the recognition site sequence is GAGACC on top (which is GGTCTC on the bottom strand oriented 5'->3' from right to left). The cleavage geometry with respect to the site will produce overhangs; perhaps in this orientation the retained gene fragment (LEFT of site) becomes the "right fragment" and site is cut away. Let's compute.

For BsaI, the recognition site is asymmetric: `GGTCTC`. The enzyme binds to a specific orientation. If the site is present as `GAGACC` on the top strand (i.e., recognition on the bottom strand), the cut positions relative to the site's coordinates flip. In Biopython, the compsite regex includes both GGTCTC and its reverse complement GAGACC. The cut coordinates charac (7,5) are defined relative to the "GGTCTC" orientation. For a site on the opposite strand, the resulting fragment geometries mirror.

`full` returns cut positions. For GGTCTC at index 7, cut at 15; for GAGACC at index 7, cut at 3. Interesting: cut position flips from 15 to 3. Since test sequences have same flanks, the top strand cut for GAGACC is at position 3, i.e., before the site. This suggests that in the GAGACC orientation, the enzyme cuts such that the retained fragment is the right part including the site? Let's analyze.

Sequence: `CCCCAAAGAGACCGGGGCCCCTTTT`; site GAGACC at positions 7-12. Cut position 3 means the top strand is cut at index 3 (between C at 3 and A at 4), which is 4 bases upstream of the site start (7). The left fragment "CCC" (positions 0-2) is tiny; the right fragment includes positions 4+ which includes the GAGACC site. Hmm this suggests that in the reverse complement orientation, BsaI cuts upstream (5' side) of the site, leaving the SITE on the retained fragment. That might be how right-end tails are designed: the site at the end gets cut away to the... wait cut at 3 leaves positions 4..24 (including GAGACC) in the RIGHT fragment dropped/retained? If the right fragment is retainedholmes, it includes the site (bad). Unless the cut position 3 is actually the OTHER strand's cut.

Biopython `full` for Type IIS may list only one cut position (of the top strand) for each site; the other cut position may be implicit (offset). For EcoRI, `full` gives one position. For BsaI with GGTCTC at 7, `full`=15 means top strand cut at 15 (ok left fragment contains site positions 7-12; right fragment starts at 15). For GAGACC at 7, `full`=3 means top strand cut at 3, leaving the site on the right fragment. That would mean in the reverse complement orientation, the top strand cut is upstream of the site. Does that make sense? Let's draw.

For BsaI recognizing GGTCTC on the TOP strand and cutting top after N1 (1 base downstream of site) and bottom after N5 (5 bases downstream), where "downstream" is to the 3' side of the top strand (right side in our drawing). If the top strand has GAGACC (which is the recognition site read on the bottom strand in the opposite direction), then relative to the top strand, the cut might be upstream. Because the enzyme's "downstream" is defined by the recognition site orientation.

Given the asymmetry, there are two useful configurations:
1. Site in "forward" orientation GGTCTC on top: cut leaves a top-strand 5' overhang on the RIGHT fragment (downstream), site removed from right fragment. Useful for creating a 5' overhang at the START (left end) of a retained fragment (the right fragment).
2. Site in "reverse" orientation GAGACC on top (i.e., GGTCTC on bottom): cut leaves the retained fragment as the RIGHT fragment but now the retained fragment starts... hmm cut at 3 is far upstream, before site, so right fragment includes site plus downstream. That would keep the site.

Alternatively maybe the "other strand" cut at position 3 corresponds to the bottom strand cut; and the retained fragment after both cuts might still exclude the site on one strand. We need a full double-strand digestion simulation to know which fragment is usable and what overhang sequences result.

[penguin] output cap clamped hard: max_tokens 256000 -> 64767 (context_window 128000, estimated input 62209)
KEY: For one-cut (Type IIS), there are two cut positions on the two strands:
- For a site in forward orientation at location L (0-based index of the site start), the cut on the current (top) strand is at `L + fst5` (position = index of first base of right fragment).
- The cut on the complementary strand is at `L - fst3`? Wait `_rev_modify` yields `location - fst3` for sites on antiparallel strand.

But `search` returns only one list; maybe it combines both cut positions for both strands? Let's read `_search` in RestrictionType to understand.

So for NonPalindromic OneCut:
- For site matches in "forward" orientation (GGTCTC), `results` = location + fst5 = location + 7.
- For matches in "reverse/as" orientation (GAGACC), `on_minus` = location - fst3 = location - 5.

For test "CCCCAAAGAGACCGGGGCCCCTTTT", GAGACC at location 7; on_minus = 7 - 5 = 2? Wait search returned 3. Hmm maybe location of group start? The regex finditer with lookahead might match at position 2? Because compsite pattern `(?=(?P<BsaI>GGTCTC))|(?=(?P<BsaI_as>GAGACC))`. The finditer over the top string should find GAGACC starting at 7. Then on_minus = 7 - 5 = 2chl. But search returned 3 (not 2). Maybe the start location in finditer is the match start plus? Let's verify by checking `finditer` on our sequence. Actually `location` could be the position of the match's start in a 1-based coordinate? The docs mention EcoRI example: pattern at 10 (0-based? In example "GAATTC" at base 10? They say cut after G so _modify(10)->11. That implies location is 0-based start index). For EcoRI site at index 10, cut=11. So for BsaI forward location+7. For our test, GGTCTC at index 7, cut=15 (matches). GAGACC at index 7, rev_modify = location - fst3 = 7 - 5 = 2. But search gave 3. So maybe location is 8? Wait maybe the finditer match for reverse_complement in the top strand starts at 5? Let's check.

Actually the regex `(?=(?P<BsaI>GGTCTC))|(?=(?P<BsaI_as>GAGACC))`—for a non-palindromic enzyme, the "as" orientation match on the top strand corresponds to the reverse complement of the site. In dsDNA, if the top strand has GAGACC, the bottom strand contains GGTCTC. The enzyme binds the bottom strand's GGTCTC; the cut position on the top strand may be computed differently. Regardless, `on_minus` = location - fst3. If location matches the top-strand GAGACC position, location-fst3=7-5=2. But observed 3 missing by 1. Let's inspect the `FormattedSeq.finditer` maybe adjusts positions. Or maybe `fst3` must be negated? For BsaI charac (7,5). In `_rev_modify`, it uses `- fst3` (i.e., `location - fst3`). If fst3=5, location - 5. For GAGACC at 7, cut at 2. But search returned 3. Let's actually test with a simpler pattern where GAGACC at location 1 and see.

Aha! FormattedSeq has a leading space, so the regex match positions are 1-based! So in `search`, `location` is 1-based index of the first base of the site. Thus for our sequence, GAGACC starts at 0-based 7, 1-based 8. Then on_minus = location - fst3 = 8 - 5 = 3 (1-based), which corresponds to 0-based position 3 (the returned 3). Great! And forward GGTCTC at 1-based 8: location + fst5 = 8 + 7 = 15, matches. Good.

Thus for a forward-oriented site (GGTCTC on top strand) at 1-based position L:
- top strand cut at L + 7 (1-based index of first base after cut).
- bottom strand cut at L - 5? No wait `_rev_modify` is used for the "as" orientation (GAGACC on top). The bottom strand cut for a forward site is not in `search` results? For a OneCut non-palindromic enzyme, `search` returns only ONE cut position per site (the cut on the current/top strand for forward matches, and the cut on the complementary strand for reverse matches). The other strand's cut position is not returned in `search`; but the overhang length is determined by `ovhg`. To fully simulate digestion, we need both cut positions. We can compute them ourselves.

Let's derive cut coordinates from REBASE and Biopython:

For BsaI, charac: fst5=7, fst3=5, ovhg=-4 (5' overhang of 4 nt).
- For a forward site (GGTCTC on top strand) starting at 1-based index L:
  - Top strand cut: after base L + 7 - 1? The `search` returns `L + 7` as the 1-based index of the first base of the right (3') fragment (i.e., the cut is between L+6 and L+7 in 1-based). Since L is site start (1-based), site spans L..L+5. Top cut is between L+6 and L+7: one base downstream (site position L+6 is N1? Actually site length 6, so one base downstream at position L+6; cut after it). Hmm earlier REBASE: top cut after first N (N at position L+6); so right fragment starts at L+7? Wait in 1-based, if site spans L..L+5 satisfying, N1 is L+6, N2..N5 are L+7..L+10. Top cut after N1 means right fragment top strand starts at N2 = L+7. Yes search returns L+7 for top cut.
  - Bottom strand cut: ? For forward site, bottom strand has reverse complement of site. The bottom cut is at N5, so right fragment bottom strand starts after N5 (at L+11 in top coordinate)? But Biopython search doesn't return it for forward. However the overhang length tells top right fragment has 5' overhang N2..N5 = 4 bases (positions L+7..L+10). So the top cut is after N1 (L+6), bottom cut after N5 (L+10). The right fragment starts at L+7 on top and L+11 on bottom (since bottom extends through N5). Wait bottom is anti-parallel; let's define right fragment on top from L+7 (0-based index?).

Let's work in 0-based indexes for clarity.

Forward site (GGTCTC) at 0-based indices s .. s+5.
Flanking N's to the right: N1 at s+6, N2 at s+7, N3 at s+8, N4 at s+9, N5 at s+10.
- Top strand cut after N1: cut between s+6 and s+7 => right fragment top strand starts at index s+7.
- Bottom strand cut after N5: bottom strand right fragment starts after N5, i.e., at index s+11 on top coordinate; equivalently bottom strand extends through N5, so the bottom strand of the left fragment includes N1..N5 (indices s+6..s+10).
Thus:
- RIGHT fragment (retained gene fragment on right of site) top strand begins at N2 (s+7) — a 4-base 5' overhang of N2N3N4N5 (indices s+7..s+10).
- LEFT fragment top strand ends at N1 (s+6); bottom strand extends through N5 (s+10) — a 4-base 5' overhang on bottom: the complementary of N2..N5.

Now, for a reverse/as site (GAGACC on top strand, which is the reverse complement of GGTCTC), the geometry mirrors. Biopython search returns top strand cut at L - fst3 (in 1-based) = (s+1) - 5 = s - 4 (0-based). So the top cut occurs 4 bases upstream of the site start? Let's use the actual example: GAGACC at 0-based 7, top cut at 0-based 3. So right fragment top starts at index 3, which is 4 bases before the site start (7). Since the site is on the opposite strand orientation, the "downstream" direction for the enzyme is to the left on the top strand. The site's reverse complement GGTCTC is on the bottom strand reading right-to-left. So the retained gene fragment relative to this cut is the RIGHT fragment, which starts at index 3 and extends to the left? Wait "right fragment" in standard left-to-right coordinates is index 3 and beyond (to the right), including the GAGACC site. So the right fragment includes the site! But maybe the site is on the fragment that gets discarded because the bottom cut is elsewhere.

Let's compute both cut positions for the as orientation. If site GAGACC at s..s+5 (0-based), Biopython search reports top cut at s-4 (0-based). What about bottom cut? For the as orientation, the cut positions are mirrored relative to the site. Equivalent to a forward site on the opposite strand: the "downstream N's" flank the site on the left (in top coordinates). Let's derive by symmetry.

For a forward site, relative to the site (top strand GGTCTC), the enzyme cuts:
- top strand: N1 (first base to the right of site)
- bottom strand: N5 (5th base to the right of site)

For the as orientation (top strand GAGACC = reverse complement of GGTCTC), this is the same site but with top and bottom roles swapped and the site read in the opposite direction. In top coordinates, the "downstream N's" relative to the enzyme are to the LEFT of the site. So the top-strand cut is just outside the site on the left, and the bottom-strand cut is 5 bases to the left? Let's set up: as site at s..s+5. To its left are bases s-5..s-1 (call M's). Symmetric to forward case:
- bottom strand cut after M1? Let's define the "right fragment" relative to cuts: For as orientation, top cut at s-4 (observed), meaning top strand's right fragment starts at index s-4 suggested. But maybe s-4 is the cut on the top for the complementary strand? Let's derive from charac:

Biopython docs: `_rev_modify` yields `location - cls.fst3` for a site on the antiparallel strand. The resulting position is the cut on the "current strand" (top) but computed as if the recognition site is on the bottom. For a site on the bottom strand recognized forward (GGTCTC), the top strand cut is at location - fst3 = s - 5? Wait location in `_rev_modify` is match start of GAGACC on top (1-based?). We saw result s0 - 4.

Maybe the `ovhg` length 4 and `is_5overhang` tells us: Both orientations produce a 5' overhang of 4. For as orientation, the right fragment's top strand starts at s-4 (0-based) and includes the site. Does it have a 5' overhang? The right fragment's top strand begins at s-4; the bottom fragment begins after some cut further downstream? Let's determine the two cut positions for as orientation by symmetry.

Let's instead reason from first principles using the digestion geometry we established. We need to design primers for Golden Gate. Let me recall the actual standard primer format for BsaI from protocols:

I've seen in iGEM "MoClo" protocols: To add BsaI sites, use primers:
- Forward: `5'- [4nt fusion] GGTCTC ...` 
No.

Let's search memory of concrete MoClo sequences: The standard MoClo "prefix" is: `GGTCTC A ...`? I recall a "Standard Assembly" of BioBricks uses prefix:
`... GGTCTC gc ...`? Hmm.

Let's recall from "MoClo: Modular Cloning" paper (2009). They use BsaI to create standard parts. The level 0 part has:
- Prefix: `ggtctc` + 3 bp? no.

Actually, standard iGEM "RFC" for Golden Gate? There is a "MoClo standard prefix" of 10 bp: `G G T C T C A G G C G C`? Let me recall: BsaI sites in MoClo are:
- Prefix: `GGTCTC` followed by `AGGCGC`? Wait.

I recall that MoClo uses 4-base overhangs, and the standard insert overhangs are:
- type "T" overhang maybe `TACT`? No.

Let me search "GGTCTCAGGCGC" in memory: I think the standard MoClo prefix is `ggtctc` + `a` + `t`? Actually there's a sequence "GGTCTCtttt"? Let's avoid relying on exact.

Instead, we can derive the primer tails ourselves from the BsaI cut geometry:
- At the 5' end of a fragment, to get a 5' top-strand overhang of 4 nt (call O5 = first 4 bases of the fragment block), the PCR forward primer's 5' tail should be:
  `GGTCTC` + `N1` + `O5`
followed by the annealing region (first ~20 bases of the fragment block starting at O5[4]? Wait O5 is the first 4 bases of the block and is part of the amplicon; the annealing region should anneal to the template beginning at the same position as the block start since O5 is actual desired sequence present in template? But O5 could be the first 4 bases of the block, which are part of the template sequence (since it's the start of the fragment block). Therefore the forward primer's template-specific portion should anneal to the block start INCLUDING O5 as the first bases; i.e., the primer sequence is `GGTCTC N1 O5 + (next ~template bases)`. Wait but if O5 is template sequence, it is part of the annealing region AND also the overhang. That's fine: the primer's 5' tail includes the BsaI site and spacer, then the blocking bases O5 are part of the primer's template-specific region (starting at position block start). So actually the primer might be: 5'- GGTCTC N O5 [rest of annealing bases] -3', where the annealing portion begins at the very start of the fragment block/clothesline. But careful: the annealing region needs to have enough bases matching the template (15-45 nt), and the Tm is computed only on the annealing (template-binding) portion. If O5 is included as template-binding, the Tm includes those bases, which are part of the template. But since O5 is at the start of the block, the template binding spans from block start onward Mendlick.

However, in PCR primer design, the 5' tail (BsaI site + spacer) is NOT part of the annealing portion and is excluded from Tm. The annealing portion should be the sequence that binds to the template. If the O5 overhang bases are template sequence and are at the 5' end of the primer, they are part of the "annealing" region? The primer's annealing region is the part that hybridizes to the template. Since O5 matches template, it does annealTm counts it. But in Golden Gate designs, the overhang bases at the junction often are NOT part of the template (they are added via the primer 5' tail). In our case, O5 might coincide with the start of the block which IS in the template (e.g., flag internal starts with GGTA). It's okay to have them in the annealing region; they still serve as overhang after digestion.

But wait: If O5 is part of the annealing regionbuildmaster, then after digestion, the fragment's top-strand start is O5, which is part of the annealed primer, not something "added". That's fine—PCR just needs to generate the correct amplicon.

- At the 3' end of a fragment, to get a bottom-strand 5' overhang of the reverse complement of the NEXT fragment's first 4 bases (call N4 = next block's first 4 bases top-strand), the bottom strand's overhang sequence (written 5'->3') should be `revcomp(N4)`? Let's verify with cut geometry.

For the LEFT fragment at its 3' end, we need the retained fragment to NOT include the BsaI site. Using a forward-oriented site placed on the TOP strand to the RIGHT of the fragment would make the retained fragment the LEFT fragment and include the site (bad). Instead we should use the as orientation (site on the bottom/top reverse) so that the retained fragment is the RIGHT fragment relative to the cut, excluding the site. Let's find the right configuration.

At the 3' end of a fragment, the amplicon's top strand extends into the reverse primer tail. The tail includes a BsaI site (as part of the top strand, in reverse complement orientation). We want after digestion:
- The retained gene fragment should be the part of the amplicon that lies on the LEFT side (the gene), and it should NOT contain the BsaI site.
- The right end of the retained gene should have a bottom-strand 5' overhang of 4 bases.

Earlier for a forward-oriented site to the right, retained gene (left fragment) included the site. For an as-oriented site, let's analyze the cuts.

Let's set up a test sequence representing a gene block followed by a reverse primer tail with a BsaI site in the "as" orientation on the top strand. We'll use Biopython to see the fragments after a double cut (if we can compute both strand cuts). Let's compute both cut positions for as orientation.

For a NonPalindromic OneCut enzyme, how to get both cut positions? The `search` returns one cut per site: for forward matches, it returns `location + fst5` (top strand cut). For reverse matches (as), it returns `location - fst3` (which corresponds to the complementary strand cut? The docs say `_rev_modify` "As _modify for site situated on the antiparallel strand"). For the forward match, the OTHER cut (on complementary strand) would be `location - fst3`? Let's infer from the `full`/`between` results.

Let's look at `catalyse` method source for OneCut to see how fragments are generated.

[penguin] output cap clamped hard: max_tokens 256000 -> 45919 (context_window 128000, estimated input 81057)
The `catalyse` for Ov5 uses `search` which returns a SINGLE cut position per site overlapping, but for double-strand cuts it returns two positions? For BsaI `search` returned a list with one position per site (not two). Yet BsaI cuts once each strand (OneCut), so search returns ONE position per site (the cut on the 5' current strand, or if site is on reverse strand, the cut on...?). For digest simulation, `catalyse` treats that as if it were a single cut position on the DNA strand, and the single-stranded overhang is handled separately by the enzyme class (Ov5). It probably doesn't model the actual bottom cut precisely; but the overhang length is given.

For our purposes, we need the sequence of the retained fragment after a single double-strand cut including the overhang. We can compute it manually from REBASE geometry.

We established:
Forward site (GGTCTC on top):
```
... [upstream] GGTCTC N1 N2 N3 N4 N5 [downstream] ...
Top: cut after N1
Bottom: cut after N5
```
Left fragment (upstream) retains GGTCTC and N1 on top, bottom through N5; right fragment (downstream) starts at N2... (top) and after N5 (bottom) with 5' overhang N2..N5.

Reverse/as site (GAGACC on top, i.e., GGTCTC on bottom):
Let's derive by mirroring. Suppose on top we have:
```
... [upstream] M5 M4 M3 M2 M1 GAGACC [downstream] ...
```
where `GAGACC` is reverse complement of GGTCTCNAP. The site on the bottom strand reading 5'->3' (right-to-left) is GGTCTC. For this orientation, the "downstream" relative to the enzyme (to the right of site on bottom's 5'->3' direction) is to the LEFT on top coordinates.

By symmetry, the cuts will be:
- On the TOP strand: cut 4 bases upstream of the site? Let's map.
- The retained gene fragment after digestion should exclude the site on one side.

Let's write with concrete numbers using our test where GAGACC at top indices 7-12 (0-based) in `CCCCAAAGAGACCGGGGCCCCTTTT`. Biopython search returned top cut at index 3 (0-based). Let's reason: if top cut at 3, then the right fragment (downstream, starting at index 3) includes the site; the left fragment (indices 0-2) is just "CCC". For a 5' overhang enzyme, the cut geometry likely:
- Top strand cut at index 3 (between indices 2 and 3)
- Bottom strand cut somewhere further to make an overhang.

Which fragment (left or right) has the 5' overhang? The enzyme produces a 5' overhang, and for the as orientation, the overhang should be on the right fragment's LEFT end (top strand overhang) or on the left fragment's RIGHT end (bottom). Given the site is on the right fragment if we cut at 3, the right fragment includes the site, which we don't want for a product. But maybe the "right fragment" after double-strand cut is actually the left side in terms of retained gene? Hmm.

Let's compute both cut positions for an as orientation using symmetry.

For a forward site, relative to the TOP strand:
- Top cut after N1 (one base 3' of site)
- Bottom cut after N5 (five bases 3' of site)

For the reverse/as orientation, top strand has the reverse complement sequence. Let's take a concrete double-stranded segment and apply the same rule but with the site recognized on the bottom. We can generate a dsDNA where the top strand includes `GAGACC`, and below it is `GGTCTC` in the bottom 5'->3' orientation. Then apply BsaI cuts on the bottom strand etc.

Let's use a simpler approach: use Biopython's `BsaI.catalyse` on a sequence containing only one site, and see the resulting fragment sequences in `r` from catalyse? But catalyse returns only top-strand fragments for the single cut (not the single-stranded overhang). It may not be enough.

Maybe we can find a paper description of BsaI primer tails online via a general web scrape of protocol text. Let's try to access a non-Cloudflare source: "protocols.io" or "Benchling" or Addgene. Search via `curl` to `bing.com/search?q=`.

Search results not giving sequences. Let's try another approach: Recall from NEBridge kit manual, the recommended primer design:

For Golden Gate assembly with BsaI-HF v2, the primers should include:
- 5' extension: `GGTCTC` + 4 nt (the overhang) before the gene-specific sequence for one primer; and for the opposite primer, the extension is the reverse complement.
Specifically:
  - Forward primer (for a fragment's 5' end): `5'- GGTCTC + [4-nt overhang] + gene-specific-3'`
  - Reverse primer (for a fragment's 3' end): `5'- GGTCTC + [4-nt overhang] + reverse complement of gene-specific-3'`

But there may also be a one-base "buffer" between the site and the overhang because BsaI cuts after N1. Wait, if the overhang is the first 4 nt immediately AFTER the site (positions 2-5), then the primer tail must be `GGTCTC N1 overhang` where N1 is an extra base between site and overhang. If the first 4 nt of the product are the overhang, then the primer tail is: `GGTCTC` + `X` + `[4 nt overhang]` where X is any single base (N1). Because BsaI's cut leaves the overhang as positions N2..N5; N1 is lost. So there is a "spacer" base between the recognition site and the retained fragment.

Actually wait: If the retained fragment starts at N2..N5 (the overhang), then the bases N2..N5 are the first 4 bases of the retained fragment. The primer's 5' tail must be: `GGTCTC N1 N2 N3 N4 N5 ...` where N1 is the spacer (lost) and N2..N5 are the overhang, followed by the gene-specific sequence (which starts at position after N5). So the primer sequence is:
5'- GGTCTC N1 N2 N3 N4 N5 [gene-specific annealing] -3'
Here N2..N5 are the overhang (part of the final junction) and are not necessarily template-specific? They could be arbitrary junction sequences.

For a fragment's 3' end (right end), the retained fragment should also exclude the site DB66. Let's derive the reverse primer equivalent.

By symmetry, at the 3' end of a fragment, we need the bottom strand to have an extension of 4 bases beyond the top strand's 3' end, and the site/spacer to be discarded beyond that. This is the mirror image of the left-end case. Since the DNA is symmetric, the same formula should apply on the opposite strand. 

Consider the amplicon's RIGHT end. The bottom strand is the one we can design via the reverse primer. If we want the bottom strand to extend 4 bases beyond the top strand (a 5' overhang on the bottom strand), then relative to the bottom strand's 5'->3' direction (which is from right to left physically), the "start" of the retained fragment is at the right end? Let's mirror the left-end logic.

For a left-end top-strand overhang, we placed a site on the TOP strand upstream (to the 5' side), and the retained fragment was the RIGHT fragment (downstream), excluding the site.

For a right-end bottom-strand overhang, we should place a site on the BOTTOM strand downstream (to the 5' end of the bottom strand, i.e., physically further right) such that the retained fragment is the RIGHT fragment relative to that site, excluding the site, and the bottom overhang extends 4 bases.

The reverse primer writes the bottom strand's 5' end. If the reverse primer's 5' tail is `GGTCTC N1 N2 N3 N4 N5` (where N2..N5 is the overhang that remains on the bottom strand), and then the annealing region, then after digestion, the retained fragment (which is the "right fragment" relative to the site on the bottom strand) would start at N2..N5 (bottom-strand overhang) and extend into the fragment. This is exactly analogous: the reverse primer's 5' tail should be `GGTCTC N1 [bottom-overhang4] [annealing reverse complement of gene 3' end]`.

Let's verify orientation by writing out.

Reverse primer (5'->3'):
`GGTCTC X O O O O AAAA` (tail + annealing)
Where `OOOO` is the 4-base bottom-strand overhang (the bottom strand sequence that will remain and pair with next fragment's top overhang).

After PCR, the amplicon's bottom strand (5' end at right) has sequence:
5' ... AAAA OOOO X GGTCTC ... 3' (reading bottom strand 5'->3' right-to-left) ?

Wait, the reverse primer's 5' end is at the bottom strand's 5' end (right terminus). The bottom strand sequence written 5'->3' from right to left is: [5' end] GGTCTC X OOOO AAAA ... [3' end toward left]? Actually the primer itself is `GGTCTC X OOOO AAAA` (5' to 3'), and when incorporated, the bottom strand at the right end has that sequence oriented 5'->3' from the rightmost base leftwards. So the physical order on the bottom strand (left-to-right) is the reverse complement of the primer. 

The BsaI site at the very right end (primer's 5' end), then spacer X, then overhang OOOO, then annealing region AAAA. After digestion, the retained gene fragment (which should be the fragment to the LEFT of the cuts) needs to have a bottom overhang OOOO extending past the top strand's end dropped.

Let's use the forward-site cut geometry on the bottom strand. Treat the bottom strand as the "top strand" for the purpose of designing the 5' extension. If the bottom strand has `GGTCTC` at its 5' end (far right), then BsaI will cut the bottom strand after its N1 (one base downstream to the LEFT physically) and cut the complementary (top) strand after N5 (5 bases to the LEFT). The retained fragment relative to this site is the "right fragment" on the bottom strand—which extends from N2.. to?? Wait "downstream" of the bottom strand's 5'->3' is toward the LEFT physically. So "right fragment" in the bottom strand's 5' direction is the part to the LEFT of the cut (physically), which is the gene fragment. It will include N2..N5 as a bottom-strand 5' overhang at its right end. And the site GGTCTC plus spacer N1 are on the small discarded fragment at the far right end. 

Thus the reverse primer's 5' tail should be:
`GGTCTC` + `X` + `[4nt bottom-overhang]` + [gene-specific annealing] 
where the 4nt bottom-overhang is the reverse complement of the next fragment's top-strand first 4 nt (because they need to anneal). Wait the bottom overhang of this fragment must be COMPLEMENTARY to the top overhang of the NEXT fragment (the fragment immediately downstream in the assembly). Since the next fragment will have a top-strand 5' overhang equal to its first 4 bases (call R4 = p..p+3 top). The bottom overhang must pair with R4, so its sequence (bottom strand 5'->3', which is written right-to-left physically) is the reverse complement of R4: `revcomp(R4)`. Thus reverse primer tail = `GGTCTC X revcomp(R4) ...`.

Great, this is symmetric to the forward primer: Forward primer tail = `GGTCTC X R4` + annealing (where R4 = fragment's own first 4 bases). Reverse primer tail = `GGTCTC X revcomp(R4)` + annealing (where R4 = NEXT fragment's first 4 bases), but careful: the reverse primer tail's overhang is on the bottom strand, written as revcomp(R4).

Wait actually maybe both can be written uniformly as `GGTCTC X [4nt]` where the 4nt is:
- For forward primer of fragment F: the first 4 nt of F's block (the junction on F's left side), because F's top-strand overhang is its first 4 nt.
- For reverse primer of fragment F: the reverse complement of the first 4 nt of the NEXT block, because F's bottom-strand overhang must pair with next block's top overhang.

But is the spacer N1 always one arbitrary base? BsaI cuts top after N1; N1 can be any base to ensure the site is followed by at least 5 bases downstream for the cut? The bottom cut requires 5 bases downstream of the site (N1..N5). The retained overhang is N2..N5 (4 bases), N1 is non-retained spacer. So each primer tail should include `GGTCTC + 1 spacer + 4 overhang`, i.e., 11 bases of non-annealing tail (6 site + 1 spacer + 4 overhang).

But users often see adapter sequences of 4 bases immediately after GGTCTC (e.g., `GGTCTCXXXX...` without spacer) because they ignore the spacer? Let's check with NEB recognition: "GGTCTC (N)1↓" means cut after first N. If the primer tail is `GGTCTC` + 4 bases, then the first of those 4 is N1 and is removed, leaving only 3 overhang bases—not enough. So there must be a spacer to yield a 4-base overhang. Unless the retained fragment's overhang is N1..N4 and the cut after N1 on top and after N5 on bottom leaves 4-base overhang N2..N5. Yes needs the spacer.

Actually wait: Could the overhang be N1..N4 instead, with N5 on the discarded side? Let's re-evaluate the REBASE diagram: Between GGTCTC and the cut arrows, there is ONE "N" column on top (cutdown arrow after first N), and FIVE "N" columns on bottom. This implies N1 is between site and top cut; N2..N5 lie between site and bottom cut. After cutting, the top strand fragment to the LEFT includes N1; bottom strand to the LEFT includes N1..N5. The overhang (bottom) is N2..N5 = 4 nt. The top strand right fragment starts at N2 (the first retained top base). So the retained fragment's top strand begins with N2, and its bottom begins after N5? Wait if top right fragment starts at N2ley, then the "retained fragment" (right fragment) top strand begins with N2..N5 (the 4-base overhang) and then gene; bottom strand begins at gene start (after N5). But N2..N5 are NOT double-stranded in the right fragment until they anneal to the left fragment's bottom overhang. So yes, retained right fragment's first 4 bases are the overhang.

Consequently, the primer tail must include one spacer base BEFORE the 4 overhang bases: `GGTCTC + X + [overhang4] + [gene-specific...]`.

Let's validate with known BsaI protocol: I vaguely recall that the BsaI site is written as `GGTCTC` and the "overhang" is the 4 bases immediately following it, with no explicit spacer. But maybe the enzyme's cut is 1 base into the site? Let's verify the cut position "GGTCTC (N)1///" from Bing: "识别序列5'-GGTCTC (N)1↓-3'" means the cut is at (N)1, i.e., after the first N after the recognition site. So yes there is an N between.

Let's try to reason more concretely with the cut model and verify by constructing a small example and mentally running digestion + ligation to see if the final sequence matches output.

Let's settle the primer tail formula.

Define a fragment amplicon (top-strand sequence from 5' to 3') with:
- 5' tail: `GGTCTC X O1 O2 O3 O4 G1 G2 G3 ...` where O's = 4-base overhang, G's = gene/block sequence after overhang.
- 3' end (right): ... gene block ends at G_last; then on bottom strand extends with 4-base bottom overhang P1P2P3P4 (bottom strand 5'->3') which is placed by reverse primer tail `GGTCTC X P1P2P3P4` at the bottom strand's 5' end.

After BsaI digestion of a 5' tail with a forward site:
- The retained fragment (right of cut) top strand starts at O1, i.e., sequence `O1O2O3O4 G1G2G3...`.
- The very first O1 is on the top strand as a single-stranded overhang (until ligation). Good.

After digestion of a 3' tail: The reverse primer tail on the bottom strand means the top strand at the right end is the reverse complement of the primer tail. Let's write the amplicon's terminal region:
- Reverse primer (5'->3'): `GGTCTC X P1 P2 P3 P4 R1 R2 ...` where R's are annealing region (reverse complement of the gene's 3' end).
- The amplicon's top strand at the right end (5'->3') is reverse complement of that: `... R'4 R'3 R'2 R'1 P'4 P'3 P'2 P'1 X' C T C T G G` (approx). So on the top strand at the very right, the sequence contains (right-to-left) the complement of GGTCTC, which is `GAGACC` (since complement of GGTCTC is CCAGAG, reverse is GAGACC). So top strand has `GAGACC` at the far right. This is an "as" orientation site. After digestion with BsaI, the retained fragment (gene) must be to the left of the cutseree, and the bottom overhang P1..P4 must extend beyond the top strand.

Let's apply the as-orientation cut. Top strand has `GAGACC` at some position. Recall Biopython search on as site at index s gave top cut at s-4 (0-based). The site `GAGACC` spans s..s+5. Top cut at s-4, which is to the LEFT of the site. The bottom cut should be somewhere to the left of the site as well, likely at s? Let's derive by symmetry: For as orientation, the bottom strand (which has the forward GGTCTC site) is cut after its N1 (one base downstream of the site in bottom's 5'->3' direction, which is to the LEFT in top coordinates), and top strand cut after N5 (five bases to the LEFT). This produces a 5' overhang on the retained fragment. Which fragment is retained? Let's calculate with concrete coordinates.

Let top strand have (right region): `... [gene end] [spacer X] G A G A C C` (as site at far right). Base coordinates on top 0-based: suppose gene's last base is at index -1 relative to site. Therefore, in the reverse primer tail design, the "overhang" 4 bases that remain on the retained fragment are determined by the next fragment's first 4 bases (call N4). - Reverse primer (5'->3'): `GGTCTC` + `X` + `P` + [gene-specific annealing reverse complement]

- **Forward primer**: `5'- GGTCTC + 1 sp + [first4 of this fragment's top strand] + [anneal to template start] -3'`
  Wait but if first4 is part of the template, including it in the annealing region makes the annealing start at the fragment's first base. - **Reverse primer**: `5'- GGTCTC + 1 sp + [revcomp of next fragment's first 4 top-strand bases] + [revcomp of this fragment's last ~N bases] -3'`.

Now primer design specifics:

For each fragment, we need:
- Forward primer: 
  5'- tail = `GGTCTC` + `X` + `[F_first4]` 
  followed by an annealing region that binds to the template, starting at the fragment's start. The annealing region should be 15-45 nt and have Tm 58-72, and forward/reverse pair Tms within 5°C.

  The annealing region must match the template at the fragment start. For egfp/flag/snap blocks, the fragment start is inside the template at a specific offset. The annealing region could begin at the fragment start (i.e., at template position offset). The tail `GGTCTC X first4` are at the 5' end of the primer, and the rest of the annealing region is the bases right after first4 in the template. So the full primer sequence is: `GGTCTC X + [first (15..45) bases of the template starting at fragment start]`. Hmm, but the first4 are included in the annealing region; the total annealing length = 4 + (additional bases). We need total annealing length in [15,45]. We should choose an annealing region of total length (template-binding part) between 15 and 45, melting temp 58-72.

  But wait: The spacer `X` — where does it come from? The primer tail includes `GGTCTC X` as non-annealing 5' extension (7 nt) NOT recognized in Tm. The first4 are both part of overhang and the beginning of the annealing region, so they ARE counted in Tm. Good.

  However, if first4 are part of the annealing region assigned Tm, but they're also "overhang", that is fine per rule "annealed to the template sequence" — they anneal to the template (they are template bases). Yes.

- Reverse primer:
  Written 5'->3'. It has:
  - tail: `GGTCTC` + `X` + `[revcomp of NEXT fragment's first4]`
  - then annealing region: reverse complement of the LAST ~? bases of this fragment's block (which appear in the template at the fragment's end).

  The annealing region (template-binding) of the reverse primer should be the reverse complement of a suffix of the fragment block of length 15-45 nt. Its Tm computed on itself (the primer sequence includes it as the 3' end).

Reverse primer for egfp:
The fragment's last bases are at template indices 711-713 (maybe). The reverse primer annealing region should anneal to the template at a position near the end such that the amplicon ends at index 713 (excluding stop). The reverse primer's 3' end should anneal to the template's reverse complement of some suffix ending at index 713. The annealing region should be reverse complement of egfp[s:e] where e = 714 (exclusive), and the reverse primer extension during PCR will include up to the end of that suffix? Let's be precise.

For a PCR amplicon spanning template positions [start, end_exclusive) = [0, 714):
- Forward primer anneals to template top strand from position start onward: its sequence (without tail) = template[start : start+L_fwd].
- Reverse primer anneals to template bottom strand (reverse complement of template) at the right end. Its 3' end corresponds to the reverse complement of template[end-L_rev : end]. When written 5'->3', the reverse primer sequence (without tail) = reverse_complement(template[end-L_rev : end]).

So the reverse primer's template-binding part is `revcomp(template[end - L_rev : end])`.

For egfp, end = 714.

Thus egfp_rev annealing = revcomp(egfp[714-L_rev : 714]).

So backbone block top-strand sequence (in assembly order) = `input[690:] + input[:210]`, length 2247.

This means:
- backbone block start = input index 690 (the base right after ORF stop). First4 = input[690:694] = "TGAG".
- backbone block end = just before input index 210 (the ORF start); i.e., last base is input[209] = "T" (last base before ATG). Block end_exclusive coordinate in the circular input: the block in circular order ends at input[209]; equivalently if we treat the linear concatenation bb_in = input[690:] + input[:210], its last 10 = input[200:210] = "AGATATACAT" etc. So the reverse primer for backbone should anneal to the input such that the amplicon ends at input index 209 (0-based), along the template's circular topology. Since input is provided linear, but circular, the backbone reverse primer will anneal near the end of the input (positions before 210) or across the origin? Let's define carefully.

For PCR amplification of a circular plasmid, a primer pair spans a region around the circle. For the backbone fragment (block = input[690:] + input[:210]), the forward primer anneals at input[690:] (near index 690), and the reverse primer anneals near input[:210] (near index 0-209) but on the other side. The reverse primer's annealing region is reverse complement of a suffix of the block ending at input index 209. Since input is circular, the reverse primer may anneal to a region that crosses the start/end of the linear representation? Let's think.

The block in circular input is from position 690 (right after ORF) going right (in increasing index, wrapping at end) to position 209 (just before ORF). In the linear input sequence, positions 0..2726; position 690..2726 are contiguous, then wrap to 0..209. The reverse primer should anneal to the template at the boundary near position 209. The reverse primer's 3' end anneals to the bottom strand at positions roughly 209-L_rev+1 .. 209 (top-strand coords). This is within the linear input near index ~180-209 (not crossing origin). Good. The forward primer anneals at positions 690..690+L_fwd. So both primers anneal within the linear input; the amplicon spans across the end/start by PCR amplification around circular plasmid (PCR on circular DNA can amplify across the origin; the primers are on opposite strands around the circle and the polymer extends through the origin producing a linear amplicon spanning positions 690→end→0→209). Yes this is standard: To linearize a circular plasmid at a given region, you choose forward primer in one location and reverse primer in another; the amplified product is the linear sequence between them around the circle (in the direction defined by forward->reverse). Good.

So for backbone:
- Forward primer annealing region starts at template index 690 (0-based) and extends rightward.
- Reverse primer annealing region ends at template index 209; the reverse primer's 3' end anneals to reverse complement of template[209-L_rev+1 : 210].

Now, the tails for backbone:
- backbone forward tail: `GGTCTC X` + bb_first4 = `GGTCTC X TGAG` then annealing to input[690:...].
- backbone reverse tail: `GGTCTC X` + revcomp(next fragment's first4). Which is the "next" fragment after backbone in assembly order? The assembly order backbone -> egfp -> flag -> snap -> backbone. But wait backbone's right end joins egfp. The "next fragment's first4" for backbone's reverse primer tail is the first4 of the fragment that follows backbone's right end, which is egfp's first4 = ATGA. So backbone_rev tail = `GGTCTC X TCAT` (revcomp ATGA), then annealing to reverse complement of a suffix of backbone block ending at 209 (i.e., template[209-L:210]).

Wait, is that correct? Let's re-check the assembly order and overhang pairing:

- backbone's RIGHT end (3' end, last bases = input[209]) joins to egfp's LEFT start (ATGA). The overhang at this junction is egfp's first4 = ATGA. The backbone's right-end bottom overhang must be revcomp(ATGA) = TCAT. Yes backbone_rev tail includes TCAT.
- backbone's LEFT start (5' end, first bases = TGAG) is joined from snap's right end. The overhang at this junction is backbone's first4 = TGAGhols. snap's right-end bottom overhang must be revcomp(TGAG) = CTCA. So snap_rev tail includes CTCA.
- backbone_fwd tail includes TGAG (its own first4, which is also the junction overhang from snap).

So the four primer tails:
- backbone_fwd: tail = GGTCTC X TGAG
- backbone_rev: tail = GGTCTC X TCAT (revcomp of egfp first4)
- egfp_fwd: tail = GGTCTC X ATGA (its own first4)
- egfp_rev: tail = GGTCTC X TACC (revcomp of flag first4 = revcomp(GGTA)=TACC)
- flag_fwd: tail = GGTCTC X GGTA
- flag_rev: tail = GGTCTC X TGTC (revcomp of snap first4 = revcomp(GACA)=TGTC)
- snap_fwd: tail = GGTCTC X GACA
- snap_rev: tail = GGTCTC X CTCA (revcomp of backbone first4 = revcomp(TGAG)=CTCA)

Now, the spacer X: What base? NEB might recommend a specific base (e.g., avoid creating restriction sites). It should not create BsaI recognition site in combination. Since GGTCTC+X+[overhang] should not introduce a GGTCTC motif. If we choose X = 'T', then tail = GGTCTCT... which doesn't create GGTCTC. If we choose X='A' etc. Also, the spacer becomes part of the discarded piecehol, not in final output dropped. We need to place it so that BsaI digestion generates the correct 4-base overhang; any base works as N1.

But wait: The "NEB requirements" for BsaI-HF v2 might require the base immediately after the site (N1) to be one of certain? Let's check. The recognition sequence is GGTCTC and it cuts at (N)1. Generally the N can be any base-tralala. To be safe, choose 'T' (common) and ensure the resulting tails don't create unintended BsaI sites.

Let's verify BsaI cut on tail `GGTCTCT [overhang4]`. With site GGTCTC followed by N1=T, then O1..O4. Top cut after T (after N1), bottom cut after O4 (after N5 = O4? Wait N5 is the 5th downstream base: N1=T, N2=O1, N3=O2, N4=O3, N5=O4). So the 4-base overhang = N2..N5 = O1..O4. Exactly.

Now we need to design the annealing regions to satisfy the Tm constraints using oligotm with the given flags.

Tm calculation: The "annealed to the template sequence" part excludes the BsaI site and spacer tail. For forward primers, annealing region is the segment starting at the fragment start (including the first4 overhang bases, which are template-derived). For reverse primers, annealing region is the reverse complement of the template suffix.

We must find for each primer an annealing region length 15-45 with Tm between 58 and 72 (per oligotm with -tp 1 -sc 1 -mv 50 -dv 2 -n 0.8 -d 500). Also forward/reverse pair Tm difference ≤ 5.

Let's use the `oligotm` tool to compute Tms and choose regions.

First, let's extract the template sequences and define the candidate annealing regions. We'll write a Python script that for each primer enumerates candidate lengths (15-45) and maybe starting positions (we can shift the annealing region to adjust Tm), computes Tm via oligotm (subprocess), and selects valid primers. We need to choose actual primers.

But note: we can shift the annealing region within the block for each primer, as long as the amplicon includes the required block with correct boundaries and the overhang tails are placed correctly. Specifically:

For forward primer of a fragment:
- The amplicon must START at the block's first base (to preserve the exact block sequence, including the first4 overhang). Therefore the forward primer's annealing region must begin at the block start (template position = block_start). We can vary its LENGTH (number of template bases it covers) from 15 to 45 (but the entire annealing region must match template). Tm depends on length and sequence. We cannot shift start because starting later would delete the first bases from the amplicon, which are required. However, we could include the first4 as a non-annealing overhang tail and start annealing at position block_start+4, if we consider the first4 as part of the primer tail (not counted in Tm). But the rule says "part of the primers annealed to the template sequence" — the first4 ARE annealed if included in the primer; if we put them in the 5' tail (non-annealing), they would not be in the amplicon unless they're synthesized as part of the amplicon. Actually for PCR, any 5' extension bases become part of the amplicon regardless of whether they anneal. They can be non-annealing (not complementary to template) or annealing (complementary). If we put first4 as part of the 5' tail (after spacer), they don't need to anneal to template (they are arbitrary junction sequences), but they DO become part of the amplicon's 5' end. The annealing region can start at block_start+4 Fitzgerald, with the first4 being non-annealing overhang added artificially綜. This would also work, and would allow us to adjust Tm by shifting the annealing start.

Which is correct for Golden Gate? Typically the 4-base overhang is ADDED via the primer tail (completely non-annealing), and the annealing region starts after it at the first base of the gene sequence that is NOT part of the overhang. In MoClo, the fusion overhang bases are arbitrary and not part of the gene, so they are part of the non-annealing tail. In our case, since the overhang bases coincide with actual output sequence bases (the first 4 of each block), we have a choice: include them in the annealing region (since they are template sequence) or treat them as tail overhang. Both produce the same amplicon when the primer includes them.

The rule: "The part of the primers annealed to the template sequence should have a length between 15 and 45 nucleotides." If we include the first4 in the annealing region, they are annealed (since template has them initially in the PCR). That seems natural because the primer's annealing region starts at the beginning of the fragment block where the template sequence exists.

But actually, when adding BsaI sites by PCR, the primer's 5' tail is the sequence NOT found in the template. The annealing part is entirely from the template. The overhang 4 bases, though they appear in the final output, are NOT necessarily present in the ORIGINAL template for that fragment at the boundaries. Wait, for egfp fragment, the first4 ATGA IS present in the egfp template (as its start). For flag fragment, first4 GGTA is present in flag template after the ATG (position 3). For snap, first4 GACA is present. For backbone, first4 TGAG is present in input at index 690. So all the overhang bases ARE template bases! In this special case, the overhang bases are the actual first bases of the fragment in its source template. So including them in the annealing region is natural and fine.

However, what about the reverse primer's overhang (e.g., TCAT for backbone_rev)? Is TCAT present in the template? The backbone template's bottom strand overhang should pair with egfp's ATGA. The bases at the backbone's right end in the output are... The bottom overhang bases TCAT correspond to the complement of egfp's ATGA. Are those bases in the input template near position 209? Let's check: At the backbone right end (just before ORF start at input 210), the top-strand bases upstream of 210 are input[?]. The output at backbone/egfp junction: backbone's last bases "TTTAAGAAGGAGATATACAT" then egfp "ATGAGCAAG...". The bottom overhang for backbone_rev corresponds to the bases that will pair with egfp's first 4 ATGA. In the final assembled product, the bottom strand under ATGA is "TCAT". Are those bases present in the input template? The input at those positions is the ORF start "ATGA...", not "TCAT". The sequence "TCAT" is NOT in the input template at the backbone's end—it's the complement of the egfp start, which is from a different fragment. So backbone's reverse primer overhang "TCAT" is NOT template-derived; it's added as a non-annealing tail. Similarly, egfp_rev overhang TACC is not in egfp template (it's complement of flag start), etc.

So for reverse primers, the 4-base overhang is never template-derived and is part of the non-annealing tail. For forward primers, the first4 overhang may be template-derived (start of the block) so it can be counted as annealing or not.

But wait, is the forward primer's overhang truly template-derived for all fragments? For backbone_fwd first4 TGAG: input at 690 = "TGAGGAT..." yes TGAG = first 4 bases of block = template-derived. For egfp_fwd ATGA = template-derived. For flag_fwd GGTA = template-derived. For snap_fwd GACA = template-derived. So for all forward primers, the first4 are part of the template sequence at the target start. Good.

So for forward primers, the annealing region can naturally be the first L bases of the fragment's template block (starting at start, including the overhang). For Tm, we count all L bases. That's fine.

For reverse primers, the annealing region is the last L bases of the fragment's template block, reverse-complemented Dropped, and the overhang 4 bases are in the 5' tail (non-annealing). So Tm for reverse is based on the last L bases suffix of the block (as read in reverse complement? The reverse primer's annealing sequence is revcomp of template suffix; its Tm is computed on that sequence).

Now, the forward/reverse primer pair Tm constraint (≤5°C apart) applies to the "melting temperature" of the annealing parts. For each fragment, the forward primer's annealing Tm and reverse primer's annealing Tm should be within 5°C.

Let's design actual sequences.

Let's write a Python script to enumerate candidate primers and compute Tms using `oligotm`.

First, let's establish the template blocks and candidate annealing regions.

For forward primers, candidate annealing region = template[start : start + L] for L in 15..45 ape. But the actual primer (excluding tail) is that sequence. For example egfp_fwd annealing candidates = egfp[0:L]. (L between 15 and 45.)

For reverse primers, candidate annealing region = revcomp(template[end-L : end]) for L in 15..45, where end = block end_exclusive. (This is the sequence that appears in the primer after the tail.)

Let's compute Tms and pick winners. We need each in [58,72], and |fwd Tm - rev Tm| ≤ 5.

Let's automate with subprocess calling oligotm. Since many candidates, we can batch? oligotm takes one seq at a time. We'll loop.

Let's first quickly compute L ranges to see Tm ranges. We'll write a script.
[tool-986] exec_command <- Compute oligotm for candidate primer annealing regions ($ python3 - <<'PY' import subprocess seqs={}; name=None for line in open('sequences.fasta'): line=line.strip() if not line: continue if line.startswith('>'): name=line[1:].split()[0]; seqs[name]='' else: seqs[name]+=line.upper() def revcomp(s): tr=str.maketrans('ACGT','TGCA') return s.translate(tr)[::-1] def oligotm(seq): p = subprocess.run(['oligotm','-tp','1','-sc','1','-mv','50','-dv','2','-n','0.8','-d','500', seq], capture_output=True, text=True) return float(p.stdout.strip()) # Blocks (top strand) eg = seqs['egfp'] fl = seqs['flag'] sn = seqs['snap'] inp = seqs['input'] bb = inp[690:] + inp[:210] frags = { 'egfp': (eg, 0, 714), 'flag': (fl, 3, 87), 'snap': (sn, 3, 549), 'backbone': (bb, 0, len(bb)), } # For backbone, template positions: block = concat(inp[690:] + inp[:210]); starts at index 0 of bb = inp[690]. # forward annealing uses bb[start:start+L]; reverse uses revcomp(bb[end-L:end]). for name,(t,start,end) in frags.items(): print('###', name, 'block len', end-start) print(' fwd Tm by length:') for L in [15,18,20,22,25,30,35,40,45]: seq = t[start:start+L] tm = oligotm(seq) print(' L',L,'tm',round(tm,2), seq) print(' rev Tm by length:') for L in [15,18,20,22,25,30,35,40,45]: seq = revcomp(t[end-L:end]) tm = oligotm(seq) print(' L',L,'tm',round(tm,2), seq) PY)
