Let's compute the exact final primers (with tails) and verify Tm via oligotm on the annealing parts.

But wait a subtlety: The reverse primer's annealing part is the part after the tail. The entire primer (tail + annealing) is written 5'->3'. The Tm must be computed only on the annealing part. We need to output full primer sequences including tailsgment.

Let's also ensure the forward primer's annealing region starts at the block start and includes the first4. For backbone fwd, if we use L=25 annealing region = bb[0:25] = "TGAGGATCCCGGGAATTCTCGAGTA". This includes the first4 "TGAG". Good.

Let's now define full primer sequences:

Tail format: `GGTCTC` + spacer + overhang4 + annealing.
The spacer: pick a base. Let's decide to use 'T' as spacer for all primers for consistency (unless it creates issues). Actually, we should check whether the spacer+overhang creates a second BsaI site etc. Also NEB might recommend avoiding GGTCTC in the spacer combination. Let's define spacer = 'A'? We can choose any.

Let's check if tail `GGTCTCT` + overhang could create any BsaI motif across boundaries within the amplified fragment. The BsaI site is GGTCTC; we need the fragment to be cut at the ends, but after digestion, the tail is removed, so the final product won't contain these. During PCR, the amplicon contains the tail at ends; no issue if there are extra BsaI sites inside? For one-pot assembly, after digesting all PCR products, you'd want each fragment to be cut ONLY at the designed ends. If a PCR product accidentally contains another BsaI site internally, the digest would fragment it further and ruin assembly. Therefore we must check that each amplicon (after adding tails) contains exactly the designed BsaI sites at the ends and no internal BsaI recognition sites (GGTCTC or GAGACC as potential sites) within the block. The blocks themselves (template sequences) should have no GGTCTC/GAGACC; let's verify. Since output has no GGTCTC, and blocks are within output, likely none. But a reverse primer tail on the top strand becomes GAGACC (reverse site); we need to ensure no accidental GGTCTC/GAGACC within the whole amplicon besides the designed ends. The annealing sequences and block sequences should be checked.

Let's check the block sequences for GGTCTC and GAGACC occurrences.

Good, no internal sites.

Now we also need to ensure the BsaI cut-sites "satisfy NEB's requirements". NEB BsaI-HF v2 may have requirements like:
- The recognition site must be followed by at least 5 bases 3' of the site for efficient cutting (because it cuts 1/5). Since our primer tails include spacer + 4 overhang = 5 bases after the site, plus annealing region, that's satisfied.
- There may be a requirement that the site is not at the very end (needs flanking bases on both sides). Our tail has 6-bp site plus 5 bases downstream and the rest; good.
- Methylation sensitivity? BsaI may be blocked by overlapping Dam methylation? Not relevant.
- BsaI-HF v2 might be a high-fidelity version requiring "N" after site? Should be fine.
- Avoid sequence repeating that could create hairpins? Not specified.

Also the problem statement: "If you aren't familiar with BsaI-HF v2 make sure to check that the enzyme cut-sites you design satisfy NEB's requirements." This likely refers to the fact that BsaI is a Type IIS enzyme requiring 4-base overhangs and the recognition site orientation. Our design follows this.

Now, there's a concern: The reverse primer tail format `GGTCTC X overhang` produces a BsaI site that, when the amplicon is generated, appears on the TOP strand as `GAGACC` (reverse complement) at the right end. For BsaI recognition, as we saw, both orientations may be recognized differently. Our design assumed the as orientation cut geometry. We should verify that the as-orientation cut indeed produces a discarded site fragment and retained gene with correct overhang. Let's simulate with a concrete constructed amplicon to make sure the retained middle fragment excludes the site and has the correct overhangs.

Let's build a small artificial amplicon for a fragment with both tails:
- Forward tail: `GGTCTC` + spacer `A` + overhang `NNNN` (say "AAAA") + gene start...
Actually let's use real sequences perhaps. But to verify geometry, we can use Biopython to compute the cut positions for the two sites in the amplicon and see what fragment results after digesting. We can simulate using our understanding of `search` (top strand cuts) and infer bottom cuts.

Let's construct a small amplicon for flag as an example and apply BsaI digestion using a custom model to verify that:
1. The middle fragment (after removing both outer cut fragments) has sequence = overhang4 + flag_block + bottom overhang (where bottom overhang pairs with snap's first4).
2. No BsaI sites retained.

Let's do this by building the full double-stranded representation? That's heavy but doable. Alternatively, use the cut coordinates from Biopython and think.

Compute cut positions for both tails using `BsaI.search` on the amplicon top-strand sequence. The amplicon top sequence is:
[forward tail]=`GGTCTC A GGTA` + [annealing region = flag[3:3+L]] + ... (flag block continues) + [reverse complement of reverse primer] where reverse primer tail = `GGTCTC A TGTC` + revcomp(flag[end-L:end]).

Wait the reverse primer tail's top-strand appearance: reverse primer 5' tail `GGTCTC A TGTC` becomes on the amplicon top strand the reverse complement `GAGACC T GACA`? Let's compute revcomp of `GGTCTC A TGTC`:
revcomp(GGTCTCATGTC) = GACATGAGACC? Let's compute. (Do later.) The top strand at the right end is that.

The forward site is GGTCTC at the start (orientation fwd). The reverse site appears on top as GAGACC somewhere (as orientation). BsaI.search will return cut positions for both: forward site cut after spacer + overhang? and as site cut upstream.

[tool-a2b] exec_command <- Model full flag amplicon and BsaI cuts ($ python3 - <<'PY' from Bio.Restriction import BsaI from Bio.Seq import Seq seqs={}; name=None for line in open('sequences.fasta'): line=line.strip() if not line: continue if line.startswith('>'): name=line[1:].split()[0]; seqs[name]='' else: seqs[name]+=line.upper() fl=seqs['flag'] def revcomp(s): tr=str.maketrans('ACGT','TGCA') return s.translate(tr)[::-1] # Choose spacer 'T' maybe X='T' # Forward primer: tail + annealing (L=18) fwd_ann = fl[3:3+18] fwd_primer = 'GGTCTC'+X+'GGTA'+fwd_ann # Reverse primer: tail + revcomp(fl[end-L:end]) with end=87, L=22 L_rev=22 end=87 rev_ann = revcomp(fl[end-L_rev:end]) rev_primer = 'GGTCTC'+X+'TGTC'+rev_ann # Amplicon top strand: forward primer sequence + middle + revcomp(reverse primer) # The template region covered by both primers: full block from 3 to 87. # Forward primer covers [3:21]; reverse primer covers [65:87] (since L_rev=22 -> positions 65..86) # The amplicon top includes forward primer (tail+ann) + template from 21 to 65 (the uncovered middle) + revcomp(rev_primer tail+ann) # Actually PCR amplicon = fwd_primer + template[3+L_fwd : end-L_rev] + revcomp(rev_primer) amp = fwd_primer + fl[3+len(fwd_ann): end-L_rev] + revcomp(rev_primer) print('L_fwd', len(fwd_ann), 'L_rev', len(rev_ann)) print('fwd primer len',len(fwd_primer),'rev primer len',len(rev_primer)) print('amp len', len(amp)) print('amp', amp) print('bsaI cuts', BsaI.search(Seq(amp))) print('flag block', fl[3:87]) PY)

Our constructed amp had forward primer = `GGTCTC` + X('T') + `GGTA` + fwd_ann; fwd_ann started at flag[3] = "GGTA..." Actually fwd_ann = fl[3:21] = "GGTAGTGGCTCCGGTAGC" (18 nt). So the primer is `GGTCTC` + `T` + `GGTA` + `GGTAGTGGCTCCGGTAGC`. But that means the primer contains "GGTA" twice: once as overhang, once as start of annealing. Actually flag[3:7] = "GGTA", and fwd_ann[0:4] = "GGTA" (identical). So tail+annealing = `GGTCTC T GGTA GGTAGTGGCT...` = site + spacer + GGTA + GGTA...—duplicated "GGTA"! This duplication is a bug in my construction: The forward primer's tail should contain the overhang sequence (GGTA) which is ALSO the first 4 bases of the annealing region, so the primer's annealing region should NOT start with GGTA again. The full primer is tail (GGTCTC X) + overhang (GGTA) + REST of annealing (starting at block position 4, not 0). Because the overhang bases ARE the first bases of the block; the annealing region starts at block start (position 0 of block) and INCLUDES the overhang as its first bases. So the primer (excluding tail) = the full annealing region = block[0:L] = "GGTAGTGGCT..." (18 nt). The tail already contains GGTCTC X, and the overhang is simply the first 4 bases of the annealing region, NOT duplicated.

Wait, then what is the purpose of the 5' tail beyond the first4? The primer sequence (full) would be:
`GGTCTC` + `X` + `GGTA` + [annealing region after the first 4] 
where the [annealing region after first4] is block[4:L]. So the total annealing region (which anneals to template) is block[0:L] = GGTA + block[4:L]. The primer's written sequence is tail(7 nt) + GGTA + rest = tail + block[0:L]. So the primer is `GGTCTC X` + `GGTAGTGGCT...` (where the first four GGTAGT? Wait block[0:4] = "GGTA"; block[0:18] = "GGTAGTGGCTCCGGTAGC". So full primer = `GGTCTC` + `X` + `GGTAGTGGCTCCGGTAGC` (the whole annealing part). I incorrectly inserted GGTA twice.

Thus correct construction: forward primer = `GGTCTC X` + [block[0:L]]. For flag with L=18: `GGTCTC` + `T` + `GGTAGTGGCTCCGGTAGC`.

Similarly for reverse primer: The reverse primer's annealing part (after tail) = revcomp(block_suffix). The overhang 4 bases (revcomp(next first4)) are NOT part of the block; they are truly extra tail bases, so the reverse primer sequence = tail site+spacer + overhang4 + rev_ann. No duplication.

Let's rebuild the amplicon correctly:
- Forward primer = GGTCTC X + fl[3:3+L] (block start).
- Reverse primer = GGTCTC X + overhang(revcomp(next first4)) + revcomp(fl[end-L:end]).
- Amplicon top = forward primer + template middle + revcomp(reverse primer).

Let's write a comprehensive verification script.

First, define blocks and primer designs.

Definitions:
- Spacer = 'T' (tentative).
- forward primer = `GGTCTC` + spacer + block[0:L]  (since block[0:4] acts as overhang and is counted in annealing)
  Total annealing = block[0:L].
- reverse primer = `GGTCTC` + spacer + revcomp(next_block[0:4]) + revcomp(block[end-L:end])
  Total annealing = revcomp(block[end-L:end]).

Wait, for reverse primer, the annealing part should be `revcomp(block[end-L:end])`. But note: the overhang 4 bases (revcomp(next first4)) is in the non-annealing tail. The rule says melting temp computed on only the part annealed to template = the `rev_ann` part. Good.

But is the reverse primer's annealing part necessarily the last L bases of the block? Yes, to amplify the correct end. However, we should ensure that the reverse primer's 3' end is the L bases immediately preceding the block end, so the amplicon ends exactly at block end (exclusive). If we choose a shorter L, the amplicon would extend past block end? No: The reverse primer anneals to a region ending at the block end; the PCR amplicon top-strand end is at block_end. If reverse primer's annealing region covers positions [end-L, end), the primer's 5' tail lies beyond. That's correct.

Now, one more subtlety for the forward primer: If annealing region starts at block[0] and includes the first4, then the "overhang" is not part of the non-annealing tail (it's part of annealing). Does the BsaI digestion still produce the overhang? Yes, because the overhang bases are part of the amplicon's block. The BsaI cut at the site will leave them as a 5' overhang regardless of whether they were in the primer tail or annealing. Good.

Now choose lengths:
Let's pick balanced lengths:
- egfp: fwd L=18, rev L=20 (or other)
  - egfp_fwd Tm = 63.27
  - egfp_rev Tm = 62.31
  - diff 0.96

  - snap_fwd = 62.18, snap_rev = 62.71, diff 0.53
- backbone: fwd L=25, rev L=45
  - backbone_fwd = 67.74, backbone_rev = 66.03, diff 1.71

[tool-621] exec_command <- Build and verify final primer designs ($ python3 - <<'PY' import subprocess seqs={}; name=None for line in open('sequences.fasta'): line=line.strip() if not line: continue if line.startswith('>'): name=line[1:].split()[0]; seqs[name]='' else: seqs[name]+=line.upper() def revcomp(s): tr=str.maketrans('ACGT','TGCA') return s.translate(tr)[::-1] def oligotm(seq): p = subprocess.run(['oligotm','-tp','1','-sc','1','-mv','50','-dv','2','-n','0.8','-d','500', seq], capture_output=True, text=True) return float(p.stdout.strip()) X='T' # Blocks (top strand, in assembly order) eg = seqs['egfp'] fl = seqs['flag'] sn = seqs['snap'] inp = seqs['input'] bb = inp[690:] + inp[:210] # backbone block blocks = { 'egfp': eg[0:714], 'flag': fl[3:87], 'snap': sn[3:549], 'backbone': bb, } # Template-specific start/end for annealing (block coords => template coords handled inside) # For each fragment, define annealing length, and overhang (fwd uses first4 of block; rev uses revcomp(next first4)) # Assembly order: backbone -> egfp -> flag -> snap -> backbone order = ['backbone','egfp','flag','snap'] def next_block_name(name): i = order.index(name) return order[(i+1)%len(order)] # choose annealing lengths fwd_L = {'backbone':25, 'egfp':18, 'flag':18, 'snap':22} rev_L = {'backbone':45, 'egfp':20, 'flag':22, 'snap':18} # Template ranges for annealing: # backbone block is bb; its forward anneal = bb[0:L]; rev anneal = revcomp(bb[len-L:]) # egfp block tpl = eg[0:714]; fwd anneal = block[0:L]; rev = revcomp(block[-L:]) -- same as block coords # flag block tpl = fl[3:87]; fwd = block[0:L]; rev = revcomp(block[-L:]) # snap block tpl = sn[3:549]; fwd = block[0:L]; rev = revcomp(block[-L:]) primers = {} for name in order: blk = blocks[name] Lf = fwd_L[name] Lr = rev_L[name] fwd_ann = blk[:Lf] rev_ann = revcomp(blk[-Lr:]) next4 = blocks[next_block_name(name)][:4] fwd_primer = 'GGTCTC' + X + fwd_ann rev_primer = 'GGTCTC' + X + revcomp(next4) + rev_ann tm_fwd = oligotm(fwd_ann) tm_rev = oligotm(rev_ann) primers[name] = {'fwd':(fwd_primer, fwd_ann, tm_fwd), 'rev':(rev_primer, rev_ann, tm_rev)} print(f'### {name}') print(' block first4:', blk[:4], 'block len', len(blk)) print(' fwd_L',Lf,'rev_L',Lr) print(' fwd_ann', fwd_ann) print(' rev_ann', rev_ann) print(' next_block', next_block_name(name), 'next4', next4, 'revcomp', revcomp(next4)) print(' FWD primer:', fwd_primer) print(' REV primer:', rev_primer) print(' Tm fwd', round(tm_fwd,2), 'rev', round(tm_rev,2), 'diff', round(abs(tm_fwd-tm_rev),2)) print() # Write to a file? just print for now PY)

Now, let's double-check one subtle thing: For reverse primers, the annealing part (the "part of the primers annealed to the template sequence") might include the 4-base overhang? In our reverse primer design, the overhang bases are between the spacer and the annealing region生的. The overhang bases do NOT anneal to the template (they are arbitrary sequence that will pair with the next fragment after digestion, not the template). So they should NOT be counted in Tm. We excluded them. Good.

For forward primers, the first4 overhang bases DO anneal to template because they are the template's first bases. Are they really "part of the primers annealed to the template sequence"? Yes, because they are complementary to the template (they are template sequence itself). But wait—if the first4 bases are the overhang and are also template sequence at the fragment start, then during PCR they anneal to the template. But is that true for the backbone forward primer? bb[0:4] = input[690:694] = "TGAG", which is template sequence. During PCR, the primer's first4 will base-pair with template top strand. So yes, they anneal.

However, in typical Golden Gate primer design, the overhang bases are NOT annealed; they're fused to the gene and the annealing starts after themovern. But since here the overhang bases correspond to the actual start of the template block, either way works. Counting them as annealing gives a longer annealing region and higher Tm. Is this consistent with how a PCR primer would actually behave? If the overhang bases are at the 5' end of the primer and are complementary to the template, they WILL anneal. So counting them is valid.

Would the "primer3 oligotm ground truth" expect us to count only the "annealing portion" and NOT the BsaI site/spacer? Yes, we count the annealing part including first4 since they anneal to template. This seems right.

But let's reconsider: Is the overhang (first4) physically part of the amplicon that remains after BsaI digestion? Yes. During PCR, before digestion, the first4 bases are present in the primer and will pair with template (since template has them). So they anneal.

Actually wait, there's a subtle issue for the forward primer of egfp/flag/snap: The first4 of the block are the first bases of the respective coding sequences. In the template (e.g., egfp template), these bases ARE present at positions 0-3 and will be amplified anyway. So yes.

For the reverse primers, could we also make the 4-base overhang anneal to the template? No, because revcomp(next first4) is not present in the current fragment's template at the end—it's the complement of the next fragment's start bases, which are not in this template. So those 4 bases must be non-annealing tail. That's correct.

Now, let's make sure the forward primer's annealing part length includes first4 and is at least 15. Our forward annealing lengths (18,18,22,25) all ≥15.

The problem statement says "Melting temperature should be computed with respect to only the part of the primers that anneal to its respective template." Since the first4 does anneal, counting them is correct. Good.
